cos x < sin x /x < 1

As x approaches 0 from the right, cos x approaches 1. So by the squeeze theorem, sin x/x also approaches 1.

Similarly, if x approaches 0 from the left (negative side), since sine is an odd function and x is negative, both sides of the inequality will still hold because cosine is even. As x approaches 0, all the terms with powers of x will vanish except for the first term, which is 1. Therefore, the limit is 1.

lim_{x→0} cos x / 1 = cos 0 / 1 = 1/1 = 1

So all three methods—squeeze theorem, Taylor series expansion, and L'Hospital's Rule—lead me to the same conclusion that the limit is 1.

\]

\]

Now, I need to differentiate both sides with respect to x. On the left side, using the chain rule, the derivative of ln(y) with respect to x is (1/y) * dy/dx. On the right side, I have to differentiate x*ln(x). That's a product of two functions: x and ln(x), so I'll use the product rule here.

The product rule states that d/dx [u*v] = u'v + uv'. Here, u is x, so u' is 1. v is ln(x), whose derivative is 1/x. So putting it together, the derivative of x*ln(x) should be 1*ln(x) + x*(1/x). Simplifying that gives ln(x) + 1.

The derivative of \( x^x \) with respect to \( x \) can be found using logarithmic differentiation.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Let \( y = x^x \).**
2. Take the natural logarithm of both sides:
   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
3. Apply logarithmic identity to bring down the exponent:
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]
4. Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
   - Left side: Using chain rule, \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).
   - Right side: Apply product rule to \( x \cdot \ln(x) \), which gives \( 1 \cdot \ln(x) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \ln(x) + 1 \).
5. So, 
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
6. Multiply both sides by \( y \) to solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]
7. Substitute back \( y = x^x \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (1 + \ln(x))
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{x^{x}(1+\ln x)}

To find the derivative of F(x) = x · e^x, I'll use the product rule.

The product rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say u(x) and v(x), then the derivative is u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x).

In this case, let’s set u(x) = x and v(x) = e^x. 

First, I'll find the derivatives of u and v individually.
The derivative of u(x) = x is u’(x) = 1.
The derivative of v(x) = e^x remains e^x because the derivative of e^x with respect to x is itself.

Now, applying the product rule:
F'(x) = u’(x)v(x) + u(x)v’(x)
       = (1)·e^x + x·e^x
       = e^x + x·e^x

I can factor out e^x from both terms to simplify the expression further:

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \), we'll use the **Product Rule**. The Product Rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is:

\[
(uv)' = u'v + uv'
\]

Let's define:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \)

First, we find the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
- \( u'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
- \( v'(x) = e^x \) (since the derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is itself)

Now, applying the Product Rule:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
       = 1 \cdot e^x + x \cdot e^x
       = e^x + x \cdot e^x
\]

We can factor out \( e^x \) from both terms:

\[
F'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
\]

So, the final derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^x \) is:

\[
F'(x) = e^x (1 + x)
\]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{e^{x} (x+1)}

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we can use the chain rule and basic differentiation techniques.

Let's proceed step by step:

1. **Rewrite the Function Using Negative Exponents:**
   
   We can express \( F(x) \) as:
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   
   The chain rule states that if \( F(x) = g(h(x)) \), then \( F'(x) = g'(h(x)) \cdot h'(x) \).

   Here, let:
   - \( g(u) = u^{-1} \)
   - \( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \)

3. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**
   
   Differentiate \( g(u) = u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \):
   \[
   \frac{dg}{du} = -u^{-2}
   \]

4. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   
   Differentiate \( h(x) = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   \frac{dh}{dx} = 2x
   \]

5. **Combine the Derivatives Using the Chain Rule:**
   
   Multiply the derivatives from steps 3 and 4:
   \[
   F'(x) = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x = -\frac{1}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

6. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Combine the terms to get the final derivative:
   \[
   F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we can use the **chain rule**. The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), then the derivative is \( f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \).

Here, let's identify the outer and inner functions:

- **Outer function**: \( e^u \), where \( u = x^2 \)
- **Inner function**: \( u = x^2 \)

Now, apply the chain rule step by step:

1. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to \( u \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u
   \]

2. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x
   \]

3. **Multiply the derivatives together**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}
   \]

So, the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{2x e^{x^2}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln(x) \), we'll use the **product rule** in calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = u(x)v(x) \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- \( u'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) (since the derivative of \( \ln(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \))

Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
Substitute the known values:
\[
F'(x) = (1)(\ln(x)) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
\]

Simplify the expression:
\[
F'(x) = \ln(x) + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\ln(x) + 1}
\]

The derivative of \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) with respect to \(x\) is found using the quotient rule. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the numerator and denominator functions:**
   - Numerator (\(f(x)\)) = \(\sin x\)
   - Denominator (\(g(x)\)) = \(x^2 + 1\)

2. **Compute their derivatives:**
   - \(f'(x) = \cos x\)
   - \(g'(x) = 2x\)

3. **Apply the quotient rule formula:**
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
   \]
   
4. **Substitute the functions and their derivatives:**
   \[
   \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

5. **Simplify if possible:** The expression is already simplified.

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) with respect to \(x\) is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we can use the chain rule from calculus.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Functions:**
   - Let \( u = x^2 \) (the inner function).
   - Then, \( F(u) = e^u \).

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   - The derivative of \( F \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
     \]
   
3. **Compute the Derivatives:**
   - \( \frac{dF}{du} = e^u \)
   - \( \frac{du}{dx} = 2x \)

4. **Multiply the Derivatives Together:**
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = e^u \cdot 2x = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{2x e^{x^2}}

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{\sin(2x)}{x} \) can be evaluated by recognizing it as a variation of the standard limit \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = 1 \). By applying this known result and simplifying, we find that the limit is equal to 2.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Express sin(2x) using a trigonometric identity:**
   \( \sin(2x) = 2\sin x \cos x \)

2. **Substitute into the original expression:**
   \( \frac{\sin(2x)}{x} = \frac{2\sin x \cos x}{x} \)

3. **Separate the terms:**
   \( 2 \times \left( \frac{\sin x}{x} \right) \times \cos x \)

4. **Evaluate each part as \( x \) approaches 0:**
   - \( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1 \)
   - \( \lim_{x \to 0} \cos x = 1 \)

5. **Multiply the results together:**
   \( 2 \times 1 \times 1 = 2 \)

**Final Answer:**  
The limit is equal to 2.

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \) is calculated by focusing on the leading terms since both numerator and denominator are polynomials of degree 2. Dividing each term by \( x^2 \) simplifies the expression, and as \( x \) becomes very large, the lower-degree terms become negligible, leaving the limit equal to the ratio of the leading coefficients.

**Step-by-step explanation:**

1. **Identify the degrees:** Both numerator and denominator are degree 2 polynomials.
2. **Divide numerator and denominator by \( x^2 \):**
   - Numerator becomes \( 1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2} \)
   - Denominator becomes \( 1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2} \)
3. **Take the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity:**
   - Terms with \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach 0.
4. **Resulting ratio:** \( \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = 1 \)

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{1}

***********************<think>
To determine the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{\ln(x)}{x} \), I begin by analyzing the behavior of both the numerator and the denominator.

As \( x \) becomes very large, \( \ln(x) \) grows logarithmically. While it does increase without bound, its rate of growth is significantly slower compared to linear functions such as \( x \).

The denominator \( x \), on the other hand, increases at a linear rate, which means it grows much faster than \( \ln(x) \). This suggests that as \( x \) approaches infinity, the value of \( \frac{\ln(x)}{x} \) will become increasingly small.

To confirm this intuition, I can apply L’Hôpital’s Rule. Taking the derivative of both the numerator and the denominator:

The derivative of \( \ln(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \), and the derivative of \( x \) is 1.

Applying L’Hôpital’s Rule, we get:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\ln(x)}{x} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{\frac{1}{x}}{1} = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{1}{x} = 0
\]

Therefore, the limit is 0.
</think>

To determine the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{\ln(x)}{x} \), we analyze the behavior of both the numerator and the denominator.

### Analysis:
- **Numerator (\( \ln(x) \))**: As \( x \) becomes very large, \( \ln(x) \) grows logarithmically. While it does increase without bound, its rate of growth is significantly slower compared to linear functions such as \( x \).
  
- **Denominator (\( x \))**: The denominator increases at a linear rate, which means it grows much faster than \( \ln(x) \).

### Conclusion:
Given that the denominator grows faster than the numerator, the value of \( \frac{\ln(x)}{x} \) will approach zero as \( x \) approaches infinity.

The given limit can be evaluated using the standard exponential limit.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

We need to find:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)^x
\]

Recall that a similar standard limit is:
\[
\lim_{n \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{n}\right)^n = e
\]

Let’s rewrite the given expression to resemble this form.

**Step 1:** Let \( y = \frac{x}{2} \), so that \( x = 2y \).

Substituting into the original limit:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)^x = \lim_{y \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{y}\right)^{2y}
\]

**Step 2:** Separate the exponent:
\[
= \left[\lim_{y \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{y}\right)^y \right]^2
\]

We know that \( \lim_{y \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{1}{y}\right)^y = e \), so:
\[
= e^2
\]

**Final Answer:**
The limit evaluates to \( e^2 \).

To find the derivative of the function f(x) = 3x² - 5x + 4, I'll apply basic differentiation rules.

First, differentiate each term separately. The derivative of 3x² with respect to x is found using the power rule, which gives 6x. 

Next, the derivative of -5x is simply -5, as the derivative of any linear term ax is a.

Finally, the constant term 4 differentiates to 0 because the slope of a constant function is zero.

Combining these results, the derivative f'(x) is 6x - 5.
</think>

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic differentiation rules.

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   
   Adding up all the individual derivatives gives:
   \[
   f'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{f'(x) = 6x - 5}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \tan(x) \), we can use the quotient rule. 

1. Express \(\tan(x)\) as \(\frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}\).
2. Let \( g(x) = \sin(x) \) and \( h(x) = \cos(x) \).
3. Compute their derivatives: \( g'(x) = \cos(x) \) and \( h'(x) = -\sin(x) \).
4. Apply the quotient rule:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2} = \frac{\cos(x)\cdot \cos(x) - \sin(x)\cdot (-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)}
   \]
5. Simplify the numerator: \( \cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x) = 1 \).
6. Therefore, 
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)} = \sec^2(x)
   \]

The final answer is:

To find the derivative of \( \sin(x) \cdot \ln(x) \), we'll use the **Product Rule** from calculus. The Product Rule states that if you have two functions, \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative of their product is:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

**Step 1:** Identify the two functions in the product.
- Let \( u(x) = \sin(x) \)
- Let \( v(x) = \ln(x) \)

**Step 2:** Find the derivatives of each function.
- The derivative of \( u(x) = \sin(x) \) is:
\[
u'(x) = \cos(x)
\]
- The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln(x) \) is:
\[
v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 3:** Apply the Product Rule.
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[\sin(x) \cdot \ln(x)] = \cos(x) \cdot \ln(x) + \sin(x) \cdot \frac{1}{x}
\]

So, the derivative is:
\[
\boxed{\cos(x)\ln(x) + \frac{\sin(x)}{x}}
\]

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

Let's assign:
- \( u(x) = x^2 \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \)

\[

\[

\[

F'(x) = (2x + x^2) \cdot e^x

- Let \( u = x^2 \).
- The outer function is \( e^u \) whose derivative with respect to \( u \) is \( e^u \).
- The inner function \( u = x^2 \) has a derivative of \( 2x \).

**Step-by-step explanation:**

2. Let \( u = x^2 \), so the function becomes \( e^u \).

The derivative of F(x) = \(\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\) can be found using the quotient rule.

- \(v' = 2x\)

   - Right side: The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1.

3. **Set up the equation**:
   \[
   \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
   \]

4. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \)**:
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
   \]

5. **Express \( \sec^2(y) \) in terms of \( x \)**:
   - Since \( y = \tan^{-1} x \), we have \( \tan(y) = x \).
   - Using the identity \( 1 + \tan^2(y) = \sec^2(y) \), substitute \( \tan(y) = x \):
     \[
     \sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2
     \]

6. **Substitute back into the derivative**:
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
   \]

\[
F'(x) = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
\]

v(x) = \ln x

\[

Since sine is an odd function, sin(-x) = -sin x, but when we divide by x, which is also negative because it's approaching from the left, so (-sin x)/(-x) simplifies to (sin x)/x again. So the limit should be the same from both sides.

But how do I actually compute this? Maybe using the Taylor series expansion of sin x would help. The Taylor series for sine around 0 is sin x = x - x^3/6 + x^5/120 - ... and so on. If we divide that by x, we get (sin x)/x = 1 - x^2/6 + x^4/120 - ... So as x approaches 0, the higher power terms become negligible, and it approaches 1.

Wait, but is there another way without using series expansion? I think using the unit circle approach would be more intuitive. Let me visualize that.

Imagine a point on the unit circle where an angle x radians intercepts the circumference. The length of the arc is x, while sin x is the height of the triangle formed by the radius and the x-axis. As x approaches 0, both the arc length and sin x get very small. But I remember that in this case, sin x becomes approximately equal to x because they are both approaching zero at a similar rate.

Alternatively, maybe using some inequalities would help here. For values of x near 0 (excluding 0), we have cos x ≤ (sin x)/x ≤ 1 when x is positive but less than π/2. Since cosine approaches 1 as x approaches 0, and the middle term also approaches something around there, by the squeeze theorem, (sin x)/x must approach 1.

For negative x approaching 0, since sine is odd and x is also negative, (sin(-x))/(-x) = sin x / x as before, so it's symmetric. Therefore, cos x approaches 1 from below on both sides, so again squeezing our function between two values that approach 1.

Another method could be using L’Hospital’s Rule because the limit initially is in an indeterminate form of 0/0 when x approaches 0. Applying L’Hospital’s Rule involves taking derivatives of numerator and denominator separately. The derivative of sin x is cos x, and the derivative of x is 1. So applying it once gives lim (x→0) (cos x)/1 = cos(0) = 1.

The limit of (sin x) / x as x approaches 0 is a fundamental result in calculus and equals 1.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Problem Statement:** Compute the limit \(\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x}\).

2. **Recognize the Form:** As \(x\) approaches 0, both numerator (\(\sin x\)) and denominator (\(x\)) approach 0, leading to an indeterminate form \(0/0\).

3. **Apply L’Hospital’s Rule:**
   - Differentiate numerator and denominator:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (\sin x) = \cos x, \quad \frac{d}{dx} (x) = 1
     \]
   - The limit becomes:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\cos x}{1} = \cos(0) = 1
     \]

4. **Alternative Methods:**
   - **Taylor Series Expansion:** Expand \(\sin x\) as \(x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \dots\). Dividing by \(x\) gives:
     \[
     \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1 - \frac{x^2}{6} + \dots
     \]
     As \(x \to 0\), the higher-order terms vanish, leaving the limit as 1.
   - **Geometric Approach:** Near \(x = 0\), \(\sin x\) approximates to \(x\) due to their linear relationship. Thus:
     \[
     \frac{\sin x}{x} \approx \frac{x}{x} = 1
     \]
   
5. **Squeeze Theorem:**
   - For small positive \(x\), the inequality \(\cos x \leq \frac{\sin x}{x} \leq 1\) holds.
   - As \(x \to 0\), both bounds approach 1, so by the squeeze theorem:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1
     \]

**Answer:** {Final Answer: "1"}

The limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} \) can be evaluated using the Taylor series expansion of \( \cos(x) \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Taylor Series Expansion of Cosine:**
   The Taylor series expansion of \( \cos(x) \) around 0 (Maclaurin series) is:
   \[
   \cos(x) = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \cdots
   \]
   
2. **Subtracting from 1:**
   Subtract the expansion of \( \cos(x) \) from 1:
   \[
   1 - \cos(x) = 1 - \left(1 - \frac{x^2}{2!} + \frac{x^4}{4!} - \cdots\right) = \frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{x^4}{24} + \cdots
   \]
   
3. **Forming the Fraction:**
   Divide the result by \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{x^2} = \frac{\frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{x^4}{24} + \cdots}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{x^2}{24} + \cdots
   \]
   
4. **Taking the Limit:**
   As \( x \) approaches 0, all terms involving higher powers of \( x \) will approach 0:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{1}{2} - \frac{x^2}{24} + \cdots \right) = \frac{1}{2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The limit is \( \boxed{\frac{1}{2}} \).

The limit evaluates as follows:

Step-by-step explanation:
When evaluating the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity for a rational function where the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal (both being 2), we focus on the leading coefficients. The leading term in both the numerator (\( x^2 + x + 1 \)) and the denominator (\( x^2 - x + 1 \)) is \( x^2 \). As \( x \) becomes very large, the lower-degree terms become negligible compared to \( x^2 \).

Thus, we can approximate:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x^2(1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}{x^2(1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2})}
\]
The \( x^2 \) terms cancel out, leaving:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = 1
\]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll use basic differentiation rules.

1. **Differentiate each term separately**:
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

The derivative of \( \sin(x) \) is calculated as follows:

1. **Apply the Limit Definition**:  
   The derivative of a function \( F(x) \) at \( x \) is given by:
   \[
   F'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{F(x + h) - F(x)}{h}
   \]
   
2. **Substitute \( F(x) = \sin(x) \)**:  
   Plugging in, we get:
   \[
   F'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin(x + h) - \sin(x)}{h}
   \]

3. **Use the Trigonometric Identity**:  
   Expand \( \sin(x + h) \) using the identity \( \sin(a + b) = \sin a \cos b + \cos a \sin b \):
   \[
   F'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin x \cos h + \cos x \sin h - \sin x}{h}
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression**:  
   Factor out \( \sin x \) and \( \cos x \):
   \[
   F'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \left[ \sin x \frac{\cos h - 1}{h} + \cos x \frac{\sin h}{h} \right]
   \]

5. **Evaluate the Limits**:  
   As \( h \) approaches 0:
   - \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\cos h - 1}{h} = 0 \)
   - \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin h}{h} = 1 \)

6. **Substitute the Limits**:  
   Therefore:
   \[
   F'(x) = \sin x \cdot 0 + \cos x \cdot 1 = \cos x
   \]

**Final Answer:** The derivative of \( \sin(x) \) is \( \cos(x) \).

To find the **second derivative** of the function \( F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \), we will differentiate the function twice with respect to \( x \).

### Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **First Derivative (\( F'(x) \))**:
   - Differentiate each term separately.
   - The derivative of \( x^n \) is \( nx^{n-1} \).
   
   \[
   \begin{align*}
   F(x) &= x^4 - 3x^3 + x \\
   F'(x) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - \frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
   &= 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
   \end{align*}
   \]

2. **Second Derivative (\( F''(x) \))**:
   - Differentiate \( F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \).
   
   \[
   \begin{align*}
   F'(x) &= 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1 \\
   F''(x) &= \frac{d}{dx}(4x^3) - \frac{d}{dx}(9x^2) + \frac{d}{dx}(1) \\
   &= 12x^2 - 18x + 0 \\
   &= 12x^2 - 18x
   \end{align*}
   \]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{F''(x) = 12x^2 - 18x}
\]

- \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

\( F'(x) = (-\sin x)(\ln x) + (\cos x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \).

Simplifying, we get:
\( F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x} \).

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is \( F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x} \).

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the **chain rule**. 

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions:**
   - Outer function (\( f(u) \)) = \( e^u \)
   - Inner function (\( u(x) \)) = \( x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate each function:**
   - \( f'(u) = e^u \)  
   - \( u'(x) = 2x \)

3. **Apply the chain rule:**
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = f'(u(x)) \cdot u'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{2 x e^{x^2}}

F'(x) = [x²*(1/x) - ln x*(2x)] / (x²)^2

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u}{v} \), its derivative is:

\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{v \cdot u' - u \cdot v'}{v^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( u = \ln x \)  
  So, \( u' = \frac{1}{x} \)

- \( v = x^2 \)  
  So, \( v' = 2x \)

Now, apply the quotient rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} - \ln x \cdot 2x}{(x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator step by step:

1. \( x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = x \)
2. \( \ln x \cdot 2x = 2x \ln x \)

So, the numerator becomes:
\[
x - 2x \ln x
\]

The denominator is:
\[
(x^2)^2 = x^4
\]

Now, factor out an \( x \) from the numerator:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^{3}}}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we can use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

\[
u(x) = e^x \quad \text{and} \quad v(x) = 1 + e^x
\]

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

\[
u'(x) = e^x \quad \text{and} \quad v'(x) = e^x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Numerator**

\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x + (e^x)^2 - (e^x)^2}{(1 + e^x)^2} = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{\frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}}

To find the derivative of F(x) = e^{x^2}, I'll start by recognizing that this is a composite function. 

First, I identify the outer function as e raised to some power and the inner function as x squared.

Next, I apply the chain rule, which states that the derivative of a composite function is the derivative of the outer function evaluated at the inner function multiplied by the derivative of the inner function.

The derivative of e^u with respect to u is still e^u. Here, u = x^2, so the derivative of e^{x^2} with respect to x^2 remains e^{x^2}.

Then, I find the derivative of the inner function, which is d/dx (x^2) = 2x.

Multiplying these results together gives the final derivative: F'(x) = 2x * e^{x^2}.
</think>

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \), we'll use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step solution:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Outer function: \( e^{u} \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate using the chain rule**:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} e^{u} = e^{u} \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
   \]

3. **Compute the derivatives**:
   - \( \frac{du}{dx} = 2x \)
   
4. **Substitute back**:
   \[
   F'(x) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
   \]
   \[
   F'(x) = 2x \cdot e^{x^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{F'(x)=2xe^{x^2}}
